## Document 377: LatAm’s silicon dividend_ tailwinds from the AI investment boom in the U.S. (2026-10-28)

Source PDF: all report pdf/LatAm’s silicon dividend_ tailwinds from the AI  investment boom in the U.S. (2026-10-28).pdf
Report source: DB Research
Date: 2026-10-28
Pages: 6
Extraction status: ok

### Page 1

Deutsche Bank 
Research Institute

LatAm’s silicon dividend: 
tailwinds from the AI  
investment boom in the U.S.

1 
Authors 
Fancisco Campos 
Chief LatAm Economist

Beatriz Garcia Nunes 
LatAm Economist

October 28, 2026 
Key messages: 
- U.S. investment has broken away from Europe and China since 2023, 
powered by AI-related equipment, software and research spending. 
- The AI CapEx boom demands commodities and merchandise that LatAm 
can supply.  
- Latin America’s export gains are real but concentrated. Copper, computer 
and electronics products provide the clearest links to the U.S. AI buildout. 
- Chile is the region’s most direct copper beneficiary. Mexico captures the 
windfall stemming from increases hardware demand and trade-diversion. In 
these cases, the AI-related tailwinds have undergirded appreciable 
improvements in external accounts. 
- The cases for nickel and zinc illustrate that the dividend is not widespread. 
U.S. imports of the former have mostly returned toward pre-pandemic levels, 
while for the latter bilateral gains partly reflect supplier substitution. 
- While expanding AI use, supply shortages and incentives can extend the 
CapEx boom, its financial viability poses downside risks. 
Another ‘American exceptionalism’ story  
Investment has become one of the sharpest dividing lines among the world’s 
core economic blocs. Since 2024, U.S. gross fixed capital formation has firmed 
up appreciably. Meanwhile, investment in Europe has broadly stalled while in 
China’s it has retreated, the drivers of which are beyond the scope of this report 
(Figure 1).  
The U.S. divergence has largely been an AI story: a burst of spending on 
computing power, digital infrastructure and intellectual property has offset 
weakness in conventional construction and housing. Indeed, total U.S. fixed 
investment is nearly 12% above its 2023 level. Within it, investment in 
information-processing equipment has risen by roughly 57%, software outlays by 
about 35%, and research and development by around 15%. By contrast, non-
residential structures and residential investment sit below their 2023 levels 
(Figure 2).

### Page 2

LatAm Thematic Research

Deutsche Bank Securities Inc. Page 2

Figure 1: The global investment divergence

Figure 2: AI-related categories lead U.S. investment

Source: US Bureau of Economic Analysis, Eurostat, National Bureau of Statistics of 
China, Haver Analytics and Deutsche Bank Research

Source: US Bureau of Economic Analysis, Haver Analytics and Deutsche Bank Research

Latin America’s commodity channel 
AI is not merely a semiconductor story. Chips require specialized minerals; data 
centers require electricity and cooling; electricity requires grids, transformers and 
storage. That chain pulls in copper, aluminum, silver, tin, lithium and selected 
semiconductor inputs, along with natural gas and uranium. Latin America has 
meaningful exposure to several of these inputs, but copper has emerged as the 
commodity with the cleanest and most economically significant regional link. 
Copper is used throughout the AI infrastructure stack, including power cables, 
substations, transformers, server boards, heat sinks and cooling systems. Rising 
U.S. copper demand has lifted imports from Chile and Peru, particularly the 
former (Figure 3). T he effect matters beyond merchandise export volumes. 
Favorable terms-of-trade have helped Chile generate a current -account surplus 
for the first time in roughly fifteen years (0.2% of GDP as of Q2 2026 on a 4 -
quarter rolling basis ) and have allowed Peru to log the largest surplus in about 
forty-five years (4.5% of GDP as of Q2 2026 on a 4-quarter rolling basis).

28 October 2026

### Page 3

LatAm Thematic Research

Deutsche Bank Securities Inc. Page 3

Figure 3: Copper epitomized the commodity link

Source: U.S. Census Bureau, Haver Analytics and Deutsche Bank Research

Not every metal is copper 
The broader mining story demands more caution, though. U.S. nickel imports have 
recovered toward pre-Covid levels, but the upswing has not displayed the same 
persistence as copper. Mexico has captured a disproportionate share of the 
bilateral improvement, yet that gain looks more like normalization than a 
sustained AI -driven break (Figure 4). Zinc tells a similar story. U.S. real imports 
stand shy of their pre-pandemic levels, and stronger purchases from Mexico and 
Peru partly reflect a reallocation away from Brazil rather than a decisive increase 
in U.S. demand (Figure 5).

Figure 4: Nickel: recovery, not a boom

Figure 5: Zinc: a substitution story

Source: U.S. Census Bureau, Haver Analytics and Deutsche Bank Research

Source: U.S. Census Bureau, Haver Analytics and Deutsche Bank Research

28 October 2026

### Page 4

LatAm Thematic Research

Deutsche Bank Securities Inc. Page 4

Mexico’s hardware advantage 
The larger non-commodity prize is in computers and electronics. U.S. imports of 
these products have sky -rocketed, while the supplier mix has shifted away from 
China. Mexico and various Asian economies have gained share on the back of this 
double-whammy impulse (Figure 6). 
This helps explain why Mexico’s non-automotive manufacturing exports, notably 
electronics, have outpaced automotive shipments to the U.S. and pressures from 
higher oil prices. The resulting non -oil trade surplus has offset pressure from the 
oil balance and helped Mexico generate a balanced current account (0% of GDP 
as of Q2 2026 on a 4 -quarter rolling basis). The external boost is meaningful, 
although it sho uld not necessarily be interpreted as bona fide  evidence of 
nearshoring, in our view.

Figure 6: U.S. computer & electronics imports going into overdrive

Source: U.S. Census Bureau, Haver Analytics and Deutsche Bank Research.

How much runway remains? 
Quantifying or mapping out a timeframe for the AI investment outlook is beyond 
the scope of this piece or our expertise. Nevertheless, we’d venture to argue that 
the capex cycle appears more likely to mature gradually than to stop abruptly . 
One reason is that the investment case is shifting from frontier -model training 
toward enterprise inference . Capacity that once served development can 
increasingly run commercial workloads for companies. That creates a path to 
revenue even if artificial general intelligence (AGI) crystallizes later than 
expected. It also spreads demand to a much larger corporate customer base.  
A second reason is scarcity. Graphics processing units (GPUs) remain tight, while 
power availability, grid connections, cooling equipment, construction capacity 
and data -center complexity limit how quickly supply can respond. Bottlenecks 
may therefore exte nd the cycle. The constraint is especially relevant for copper 
28 October 2026

### Page 5

LatAm Thematic Research

Deutsche Bank Securities Inc. Page 5

and power infrastructure, where projects have long lead times and permitting 
hurdles. 
A third reason is strategic. AI development resembles a prisoner’s dilemma at 
both the corporate and national levels. Each participant may worry about costs, 
safety and control, yet slowing unilaterally risks surrendering technological, 
commercial or natio nal security advantages. The same tension is visible in calls 
for guardrails alongside continued spending. Competition can therefore sustain 
capex even when short-term returns are uncertain. 
We acknowledge the risks, nonetheless. Higher borrowing costs raise the hurdle 
rate for an infrastructure buildout financed partly through debt. More important, 
revenues must eventually justify the installed capital base. The bearish case is not 
that AI la cks value; it is that infrastructure spending may run ahead of 
monetization, compromising some companies’ repayment capacity. Public 
opposition to the development of AI infrastructure and/or adoption of AI 
technologies could also slow down the technology’s roll-out. 
The regional balance  
Summing up, the AI investment boom in the U .S. is delivering a dividend to parts 
of Latin America, not the region as a whole. Chile and Peru benefit through copper 
prices and export volumes. Mexico benefits through electronics manufacturing 
and the replacement of Chinese supply. For Brazil and Colombia, the AI dividend 
is rather muted. Where materialized, furthermore, the dividend supports trade 
and current accounts more clearly rather than improving the domestic 
investment. 
These dynamics should persist while U.S. AI investment remains supply -
constrained and strategically protected. If widespread adoption advances and 
the strategic race intensifies, the impulse can last. If financing costs rise further 
or monetization disappo ints, the cycle may narrow. Either way, copper and 
Mexican electronics remain the region’s most visible gauges of the U.S. AI 
investment appetite.

28 October 2026

### Page 6

Deutsche Bank Research Institute 
LatAm’s silicon dividend: tailwinds from the AI investment boom in the U.S. – October 28, 2026

## Document 394: Preview of 2026 New Zealand General Election (2026-10-30)

Source PDF: all report pdf/Preview of 2026 New Zealand General Election (2026-10-30).pdf
Report source: Westpac IQ
Date: 2026-10-30
Pages: 13
Extraction status: ok

### Page 1

1
Westpac New Zealand Economics
• Polling points to a very close and unusually
fragmented election, with the outcome
likely to depend on the performance of the
Opportunity Party and whether any party
achieves “overhang” seats.
• While either Labour or National will lead the
next government, minor parties are likely to
have unprecedented representation in the next
government. This makes it harder to assess
the mix of policies that will emerge from

coalition negotiations.
• Compared to the status quo, a returned
National-led government with stronger
ACT and NZ First representation may be
more aggressive on some combination
of expenditure restraint, welfare and
immigration, resource development
and deregulation.
• A Labour-Green-Opportunity administration
would probably deliver more public-service
and social investment, a broader tax base,
stronger climate and labour regulation and
likely greater borrowing for infrastructure.

A centre-left government may find it hard to
resist pressure to lift public service salaries
and the minimum wage in response to fuel-
driven inflation.
• The fiscal starting point acts as a constraint:
the PREFU projects that OBEGALx will remain
in deficit until 2028/29 and achieving that
outcome will require exceptional spending
control in the current environment.
• Whichever government is elected will need
to make hard trade-offs, or raise additional

revenue or debt. The latter will be costly with
global investors requiring higher interest rates
to fund government (and other) debt.
• A key non-fiscal outcome of a change
in government would likely be the
implementation of the Labour Party’s
proposal to restore the RBNZ’s dual inflation-
employment mandate.
The election appears close, but the bigger
story is fragmentation.
New Zealand goes to the ballot box on 7 November
(although advance voting started on 26 October). The

campaign is taking place against a mixed economic
backdrop: activity has recovered somewhat, but
households remain sensitive to high living costs and a
weak housing market; the fiscal accounts have improved
relative to the Budget but still show deficits until 2028/29.
That combination gives the various parties room to
argue about priorities, but little room to avoid difficult
trade-offs. Additional spending – which may be difficult
to avoid in the current environment – will require either

additional revenue or a willingness to accept additional
debt issuance at a time when international investors are
seeking greater compensation to fund government debt
(and other).
The striking feature of this campaign is the reduced
dominance of the two major parties. Across the major
polls, neither traditional centre-left or centre-right
blocs can be confident of governing alone, and the
composition of the minor-party vote will determine both
who governs and how. Because the Opportunity Party

sits near the threshold, a movement of only a few tenths
of a percentage point could alter the allocation of several
seats. In addition, any “overhang” created by Te Pāti Māori
or the National Party winning more electorates than their
party vote entitlement could also move the majority line
above the usual 61 seats.
ECONOMIC BULLETIN
Preview of 2026 General Election.
27 Oct 2026 |  Westpac Economics Team | westpac.co.nz/economics | economics@westpac.co.nz

Kelly Eckhold, Chief Economist | +64 9 348 9382 | +64 21 786 758 | kelly.eckhold@westpac.co.nz

### Page 2

2
Westpac New Zealand Economics
Together with uncertainty about voter turnout –
especially considering changes in voter registration rules
– the outcome of this General Election is difficult to call.
With major-party support unusually low and the minor
parties offering distinct, sometimes internally conflicting
programmes, the shape of the next government will be
determined not only at the ballot box on 7 November,
but at the negotiating table afterwards. Moreover, if the

results are especially tight, the outcome may not be clear
until sometime after the official results are declared on
27 November. And a hung parliament – where neither the
centre-left nor centre-right bloc can command a majority
– cannot be entirely ruled out either.
How New Zealand’s electoral system works.
New Zealand’s electoral system uses Mixed Member
Proportional representation (or MMP). Voters receive
two votes: an electorate vote for a local candidate and a

party vote for a political party. The electorate candidate
with the most votes wins the local seat. The party vote is
the main determinant of each qualifying party’s overall
representation in Parliament.
Parliament normally has 120 seats. For the 2026
election there are 71 electorates, comprising 64 general
electorates and seven Māori electorates, with the
remaining seats ordinarily filled from party lists. A party
qualifies for proportional representation by receiving

at least 5% of the nationwide party vote or winning an
electorate seat. List MPs are then added to electorate
MPs so that each qualifying party’s total representation
broadly matches its party-vote share.
An overhang arises when a party wins more electorate
seats than its party vote would otherwise entitle it to.
The party keeps those seats and Parliament expands,
which can raise the number required for a majority.
Government formation is separate from the allocation of

seats: a grouping must demonstrate that it can command
confidence in the House and pass supply. That can be
achieved through a formal coalition, a confidence-and-
supply agreement, or other support arrangements. The
largest party is not automatically guaranteed office and
minority governments are possible (e.g. although the
National Party secured more than 44% of the vote in the
2017 election, a minority Labour Party-NZ First coalition
was formed, supported by a confidence-and-supply

arrangement with the Green Party).
The overhang risk is unusually important in 2026 because
current polling leaves both blocs close to, but often
short of, a majority. In addition, the fragmentation of
the electorate increases the risk that one of the major
parties wins more electorates than implied by its party-
vote entitlement. This is in addition to the likelihood that
Te Pāti Māori will achieve an overhang given that it is
running an “electorate vote only” campaign and so asking

its supporters to cast their party votes for other left-
leaning parties. Each additional overhang seat expands
Parliament and so lifts the threshold needed to command
a majority.
The direction of the effect depends on which party
creates the overhang. Extra Te Pāti Māori electorate seats
would add MPs aligned more naturally with a Labour-
Green administration and could make Te Pāti Māori
support or abstention more consequential. A National
overhang would add seats on the right and could improve

the arithmetic for a National-ACT-NZ First government.
Under the latest RNZ-Reid Research projection, for
example, an overhang would arise if National won more
than 32 electorates or Te Pāti Māori won more than two.
This makes the party vote strategically important and
explains the focus on Opportunity’s threshold position.
A vote share just below 5% would produce no list seats
unless the party won an electorate (which based on
limited polling presently seems unlikely). A result just

above 5% would give it a parliamentary caucus large
enough, on current polling, to become the “kingmaker”
and decide which side can form a government. While
the Opportunity Party says that it could work with either
a centre-left or centre-right government, as detailed in
the appendix its broad policy platform might imply that it
more naturally aligns with the centre-left.
What the polls imply.
Kiwipoll’s six-poll tracker, last updated on 20 October,

put Labour on 27.9% and National on 28.7% - a gap
of just 0.8 percentage points. More strikingly, the two
major parties together attracted only 56.6%. The central
message across all polls is therefore fragmentation:
neither major party is close to governing alone, and almost
half of the represented vote is distributed among parties
that would shape the terms of any governing arrangement.
On that average, the balance between the established
blocs is also extremely narrow. The model associated

with the tracker assigned 56 seats to Labour, the
Greens and Te Pāti Māori, and 61 to National, ACT and
New Zealand First. Neither side reached a majority in a
presumed 124-seat parliament. Opportunity’s average
support of 6.2% translated into seven seats, enough to
take either bloc to the 63-seat threshold needed for a
majority. The average therefore implies that the identity
of the largest party may matter less than whether
Opportunity remains above 5% and which grouping it is

prepared to support.
This is not simply a close two-bloc contest. The polling
average also suggests that the minor parties would enter
negotiations with unusually large leverage. The Greens,
ACT and New Zealand First would consequently have
more scope than usual to influence tax, expenditure,
regulation, immigration and resource policy, or the
allocation of Cabinet positions, making it harder to
determine the likely form of a final coalition agreement.

### Page 3

3
Westpac New Zealand Economics
That said, the leverage that these parties can apply on
their major party coalition partner is limited. The Green
Party cannot credibly threaten that it would form an
alternative coalition with the National Party. And neither
could ACT or NZ First credibly threaten to form an
alternative coalition with the Labour Party (while NZ First
has worked with the Labour Party in the past, it has ruled
out doing so on this occasion). Moreover, it is possible

that minor parties would be punished by the electorate
if a failure to form a government necessitated a second
general election, especially if the minor parties were
perceived to have made unreasonable demands or if the
electorate simply refocused on establishing a workable
parliamentary majority.
The Opportunity Party is the pivotal uncertainty because
its average position is above the threshold, but not
far above it. A result modestly below 5%, without an

electorate victory, would remove its votes from the
seat allocation and substantially change the arithmetic,
favouring the centre-right bloc. A result around its recent
6% polling average (or higher) would make the party
central to government formation.
Formally, National has ruled out working with
Opportunity, due to its objection to the latter’s tax policy.
For its part, Opportunity has said that it would talk first to
the party with the most seats, which on present polling

could be either Labour or National. However, as noted
previously, given Opportunity’s policy orientation, it
seems more likely that it would ultimately join a centre-
left coalition than a centre-right coalition.
To summarise, the bloc contest is close, and the route
to a majority depends on threshold and electorate
outcomes that remain uncertain. With that in mind, we
think that the following two scenarios are the most likely
outcomes of the election.

Scenario one: a returned centre-right
government, with greater minor party
representation.
The first scenario is that the existing coalition is returned,
albeit with the minor parties gaining strength and thus
additional leeway to pursue their policy agendas. We
assume that the coalition is not dependent on the
Opportunity Party to govern, which would be more likely
to occur if the latter fell marginally short of the party
vote 5% threshold needed to enter parliament in the

first place.
A renewed National-led government would begin with
considerable continuity. National’s fiscal plan retains
the current coalition’s focus on restoring surplus,
reducing debt as a share of GDP , keeping new spending
constrained and making greater use of planning reform
and private capital. It projects an OBEGALx surplus of
$4 billion in 2028/29, with net core Crown debt peaking
at 43.9% of GDP in 2027/28 and falling to 39.5% by
2030/31. National has ruled out new tax cuts during the

next term, saying tax relief should wait until the books
are back in surplus and surpluses are growing. But a
coalition in which ACT and New Zealand First held a
larger share of seats would be different from the current
more National-dominated administration. Unless its vote
share is particularly low, National would almost certainly
still provide the Prime Minister (sharing the role with NZ
First and ACT in the context of a three-year electoral
term seems quite unlikely – the latter will likely share the

Deputy role as they have in the current term). The three
parties share a common fiscal framework at the macro
level. However, National would have less ability to defer or
dilute its partners’ priorities.
Fiscal policy and the size of government.
ACT would press for a faster and deeper reduction in
the state’s footprint. Its fiscal plan proposes cutting
$9.5bn across the forecast horizon from spending outside
welfare and protected frontline services, reducing future

operating allowances by $400m a year to $2.0bn, and
moving non-welfare spending back towards 2017 real per-
capita levels. ACT says this would lower debt by $12.4bn
by 2031 relative to current forecasts. It also proposes
consolidating government into 19 departments and
18 ministers.
On its own, that agenda would pull a renewed
government beyond National’s more incremental
expenditure control. Coalition negotiations would likely
turn on the pace of implementation and the definition

of protected frontline services. The economic effect
would depend on whether savings represented genuine
efficiency gains or reductions in service capacity and
transfers. In the near term, a faster consolidation could
subtract from demand, while lower debt issuance
and a smaller structural deficit would strengthen the
fiscal buffer.
However, New Zealand First would push in a different
direction. Its programme combines selective tax relief
with active government intervention, regional grants

and strategic ownership. The tension within the coalition
would therefore not simply be between ‘more’ and ‘less’
government. ACT would seek a smaller and more market-
oriented state; New Zealand First would accept a larger
role where it supported national ownership, regions,
families or strategic industries. New Zealand First’s
proposal to reduce the corporate tax rate for SME’s to
20% (from 28%) may well be accepted by its coalition
partners during coalition negotiations – possibly phased

in over time – and would certainly be much cheaper
than the fiscal cost of some other policies promoted
by the party (notably compensating employees for
higher compulsory Kiwisaver contributions and the
nationalisation of the Bank of New Zealand).

### Page 4

4
Westpac New Zealand Economics
Tax, welfare and retirement.
ACT’s longer-run objective is a lower and flatter tax
system. Its immediate plan removes tax on qualifying
KiwiSaver investment earnings, ends the government
KiwiSaver contribution and returns net Emissions Trading
Scheme auction revenue to households through a
carbon-tax refund. Broader tax relief is deferred until
the books are balanced. ACT would raise the NZ Super
eligibility age by three months a year to 67 by 2035, while

allowing compassionate early access for people with long
careers in physically demanding work. It would also pause
contributions to the NZ Super Fund.
On welfare, ACT proposes tighter medical certification
and reassessment for disability-related benefits, a five-
year stand-down before migrants can access welfare,
and lower spending outside protected services. NZ
First would add its own eligibility restrictions, including
limiting NZ Super to citizens from 2029, while proposing

compulsory KiwiSaver enrolment from birth and a Crown
contribution. These policies would create difficult design
questions around transitional arrangements, hardship
and administration, but the broad direction would be
towards tighter eligibility and stronger conditionality.
Regulation, resources and labour markets.
A stronger ACT-NZ First presence would reinforce
deregulation in planning, employment and resource
development. ACT would seek property-rights-based

planning law, easier dismissal, a three-year minimum
wage freeze, a lower training wage for workers under
20 and removal of Te Mana o te Wai from consenting.
It would repeal the Zero Carbon Act. National’s own
RMA replacement and fast-track agenda would provide
substantial common ground.
NZ First would push more directly for regional and
strategic development: a special economic zone at
Marsden Point, offshore oil and gas surveying, expanded
mineral development, and the return of 50% of mining

royalties to producing regions. It also proposes breaking
up the power gentailers and has advocated bringing a
major bank back into New Zealand public ownership.
Both the National Party and NZ First have policies that
aim to force additional competition in the supermarket
sector, but these are opposed by the ACT Party.
Nonetheless, the result could be a government that is
simultaneously more deregulatory in consenting and
more interventionist in ownership and competition policy.

Immigration would also tighten. ACT proposes annually
reviewed work-visa categories, a Rural Workforce Visa,
stronger overstayer enforcement and physical-presence
requirements for permanent residents. NZ First proposes
replacing the Accredited Employer Work Visa with a
shortage-led Skilled Work Visa and holding a referendum
on a 20,000 annual cap on resident visas. National’s more
skills-focused approach would be under pressure from
both partners to place greater weight on enforcement,

settlement obligations and lower aggregate inflows.
Scenario two: Labour and the Greens,
supported by Opportunity.
The second main scenario that we view as likely is a
Labour Party-led coalition with the Green Party, with the
Opportunity Party supporting either inside the coalition
or via a confidence-and-supply arrangement. Depending
on precise outcome of the election, a confidence-and-
supply arrangement may also be required with Te Pāti
Māori to secure the necessary parliamentary majority.

A Labour-led alternative would represent a clearer break
with the current policy direction, but the size of that break
would depend on the success of the minor partners in
embedding their policies in the coalition agreement.
For its part, the Labour Party would aim to restore and
expand selected public services while remaining inside
its published fiscal framework. Its plan retains a $2.4
billion operating allowance for each Budget, targets an
OBEGAL surplus in 2028/29 and seeks to reduce net

debt below 20% of GDP over time. It would introduce
a 28% capital gains tax on post-1 July 2027 gains from
investment residential and commercial property, with
major exemptions, and use the revenue to help fund
health initiatives.
Labour’s central commitments include free prescriptions,
three free doctors’ visits, additional screening and
maternity services, 6,000 additional state houses,
public-transport fare caps, expanded apprenticeships, a
care-worker pay increase and partial reversal of planned

public-service cuts.
It would also restore the Reserve Bank’s dual price-
stability and maximum-sustainable-employment
mandate. As we have discussed in detail in a previous
note, in the current environment – dominated by supply
shocks – the return of the dual mandate could mean that
the RBNZ is prepared to accommodate a slower return of
inflation to target to reduce the impact on employment.
The Greens would shift the centre of gravity.
A strong representation of Green Party ministers in

the Cabinet would seek to move a coalition further on
redistribution, public ownership, housing and climate
policy. The party’s tax programme includes a 2.5% wealth
tax on net assets above $10 million excluding the family
home, a capital-acquisitions tax above $1 million with
exemptions, a 45% top personal rate, a higher company
tax rate for the largest firms, and a levy on large banks.
The stated objective is to raise about $5.1 billion net and
shift the tax burden from wages towards wealth and

large corporations.

### Page 5

5
Westpac New Zealand Economics
These taxes are needed to finance the Green Party’s
extensive promises on spending (see below). Formally,
the Labour Party has explicitly ruled out supporting the
Green Party’s tax policies to secure a Labour Party-led
coalition. Whether that pre-election commitment would
be reversed after the election is a matter of conjecture.
On spending and ownership, the Green Party proposes
free dental care and public transport, stronger income

support, expanded school lunches, more public housing,
a publicly owned renewable-energy entity and the
acquisition of supermarket stores to establish KiwiMart.
A centre-left government, especially with strong Green
Party representation, may find it harder to resist pressure
to lift public service salaries and the minimum wage
in response to fuel-driven inflation. Relative to PREFU
forecasts, this would imply additional expenditure that
would need to be met with either additional sources of

revenue or additional debt issuance.
Labour would resist parts of this agenda on cost,
implementation risk and fiscal credibility. The most likely
coalition bargain would therefore be a narrower set of
high-priority Green Party reforms, financed by some
combination of new revenue, reprioritisation and the
available Budget allowances.
The regulatory direction would be unambiguously
stronger. Labour and the Greens overlap on restoring
pay equity (which was not explicitly budgeted for in

Labour’s fiscal plan), strengthening worker protections,
improving rental standards, expanding public housing
and using government more actively in markets with
weak competition. Climate, freshwater and biodiversity
rules would tighten relative to the current government,
and mining on conservation land would face much
greater barriers.
Opportunity’s role, potentially outside Cabinet.
Opportunity could support a Labour-Green government,
either directly joining the coalition or through a confidence-

and-supply agreement. While the former would give
Opportunity seats at the Cabinet table, the latter
arrangement would let the party retain a distinct identity,
trading reliable support on confidence and budgets for a
defined policy programme. It may also provide more scope
for the party to “learn the ropes” with less public scrutiny,
given that none of its prospective MPs have ever been in
Parliament, let alone held Cabinet positions.
Regardless of what form the arrangement takes,

Opportunity’s landmark policies are neither a simple
extension of Labour nor of the Greens. Opportunity
proposes a “tax reset”, including a land value tax, with a
lower rate for rural land, alongside personal income tax
rates of 28%, 34% and 39%. The revenue raised would be
used to fund a “Citizen’s Income” payment to almost all
people, which would also replace several current social
welfare benefits. That said, Opportunity has conceded
that the land tax and Citizen’s Income are unlikely to be

implemented in the next Parliament given that it will
probably be the smallest of the coalition partners.
In negotiations, Opportunity would press for the
inclusion of its five bottom lines. These are (1) giving
the Commerce Commission more teeth and greater
independence to tackle excessive market power in
supermarkets and other uncompetitive sectors, (2)
regulating lobbying and capping political donations,
(3) developing a 10-year plan to "fix the health system"

alongside health professionals, with clear commitments
on funding, workforce and infrastructure, (4) phasing
out bottom trawling, working with industry, iwi and
coastal communities to fairly manage the transition,
(5) recommitting to Paris Agreement emissions
reduction obligations and protecting the Climate Change
Commission's independence. Opportunity’s other policies
include allowing net debt to exceed 50% of GDP if the
additional borrowing finances productive infrastructure.

It also proposes compulsory KiwiSaver contributions
rising to 6% for employees and employers (a policy it
shares with National), free public transport, stronger
competition enforcement, higher R&D spending and a
ten-year cross-party education plan. In a confidence
and supply arrangement Opportunity could reserve the
right to oppose other legislation it disagrees with, but
the arrangement would need sufficiently clear fiscal
guardrails to avoid repeated uncertainty over Budgets

and concerns about the stability of the Government.
The economic stakes.
The two scenarios imply meaningfully different policy
mixes, but neither government would operate without
constraints. The current fiscal forecasts already assume
a return to surplus and a declining debt ratio later in the
forecast period. Delivery depends on tight expenditure
control, revenue growth and an economic outlook
exposed to oil prices, global interest rates and domestic

productivity. Coalition agreements that materially add
to operating spending or reduce revenue would need
offsetting measures if the party heading the coalition
wished to preserve its stated fiscal track.
For monetary policy, the direct institutional contrast is
Labour’s proposal to restore the Reserve Bank’s dual
mandate. In practice, the larger near-term influence may
come through fiscal demand, labour market regulation,
migration and energy policy. A faster fiscal consolidation

on the right would tend to reduce demand pressure, while
a larger operating spending programme on the left would
increase demand pressure. Infrastructure investment
is somewhat different: if well selected and delivered, it
can lift productive capacity over the medium term, but
it can also add to near-term capacity pressure before
supply benefits arrive. And given the current interest rate
environment, the hurdle that must be crossed to justify
raising debt is higher than it has been for years.

### Page 2

The Week Ahead October 30, 2026 
                                                                                                                                         2

CENTRAL BANK MEETINGS

Bank Negara Malaysia (BNM)  (5 
November)

As written in our Macro Strategy note , 
BNM’s final meeting of 2026 on 5 
November will be live and a close call, 
after the central bank opened the door to 
monetary policy normalisation during its 
September review. BNM Governor Abdul 
Rasheed Ghaffour reiterated in a 
Bloomberg interview on 15 Oc tober that 
the central bank has space to adjust 
policy if needed, as policymakers are 
vigilant about upside inflationary 
pressures amid strong economic growth. 
He also said that policy decisions will be 
data dependent. We think the latest 
available Septemb er data for the 
upcoming meeting suggest that 
policymakers can afford to be patient 
until 1H27, maintaining the Overnight 
Policy Rate at 2.75% for now. Inflation 
remained contained within the lower half 
of the official 2026 inflation forecast 
range of 1.5 -2.5%, notwithstanding 
external price pressures and robust 
economic activity. Advance estimates 
showed GDP growth of 5.3% yoy in 3Q26, 
while exports jumped to a five -year high 
of 54.5% yoy in September, 
supercharged by the artificial 
intelligence boom. However, headline 
inflation eased slightly to 1.8% yoy in 
September 2026 from 1.9% yoy in August, 
while core inflation remained stable at 1.7% 
yoy. Fuel subsidies have limited the pass-
through of upstream cost pressures to 
consumer prices, alongside stable 
domestic demand conditions.

FORTHCOMING DATA 
RELEASES

South Korea

October trade and inflation data are due 
for release. Export growth is expected to 
accelerate to nearly 100% yoy, up from 
83.7% in September, while the trade 
surplus is forecast to remain above 
USD40bn for another month. CPI 
inflation is expected to hold steady at 2.9% 
yoy. Overall, the data should reinforce 
the view that AI - and semiconductor -
driven growth and the expansion in 
South Korea’s trade surplus remain 
firmly on track, providing fundamental 
support for the KOSPI and KRW. 
Meanwhile, inflation remains sticky 
above the BOK’s 2% target, bolstering 
the case for further monetary tightening 
as early as the November policy meeting.

Taiwan

October inflation data are forthcoming. 
Headline CPI inflation is expected to 
remain above 2% for the sixth 
consecutive month, at 2.3% yoy. Core 
CPI inflation is also likely to stay above 2% 
for the sixth consecutive month, at 2.3% 
yoy. Despite government subsidies on 
fuel and electricity prices, higher global 
energy prices continue to feed through 
to CPI via items such as airfares and tour 
package fees. Services inflation also 
remains sticky, particularly in food away 
from home, housin g, transportation, and 
entertainment. Meanwhile, domestic 
inflation expectations appear to be 
gradually rising. A recent private -sector 
survey showed that the public expects

### Page 3

The Week Ahead October 30, 2026 
                                                                                                                                         3

inflation to average 2.5% in 2026, with 
52% of respondents expecting inflation 
to exceed 2.5%. Against this backdrop, 
we maintain our view that the central 
bank will resume rate hikes in December, 
raising the policy rate by 12.5  bps to 
2.125%.

Thailand

We expect Thailand’s headline inflation 
to rise to 3.2% yoy  in October 2026, 
extending the increase over the previous 
two months, and moving slightly above 
the upper end of the Bank of Thailand’s 
1-3% target range. This was likely driven 
by faster energy inflation amid an 
uptrend in diesel prices, alongside a 
rebound in food inflation. The inflation 
uptrend was likely broadly in line with 
the central bank's expectations and 
mainly supply-side driven, supporting its 
decision to look through the temporary 
price pressures and keep its policy rate 
unchanged at its October meeting.

Vietnam

We expect Vietnam’s October 2026 
goods exports and retail sales to remain 
robust at 42.0% yoy and 14.0% yoy year-
to-date, respectively, while headline 
inflation is likely to stay elevated at 
around 5.0% yoy. Exports have 
continued to be supported by strong  
electronics and machinery shipments 
amid a favourable technology cycle with 
spillovers from robust artificial 
intelligence-related demand. Retail 
sales were likely bolstered by firm 
household spending, supported by 
favourable labour market conditions, 
and continued strength in tourism. 
Headline inflation has remained elevated 
and above the central bank’s target of 
4.5% for the year, driven by price 
pressures from transport and energy, 
food, as well as material costs. This 
poses a challenge for policymakers as 
they seek to balance inflati on risks 
against ambitions of achieving at least 10% 
economic growth this year.

Hong Kong

Hong Kong’s economy is expected to 
decelerate from 4.3% yoy in Q2 to 2.6% 
in Q3, largely due to slower private 
consumption. Despite a 13% increase in 
Mainland visitor arrivals during the 
period, domestic consumption continued 
to be weighed down by a weaken ing 
labour market, with the unemployment 
rate elevated at 3.9% in September. 
Meanwhile, higher interest rates have 
dampened investment sentiment and 
capital market activity. The Centa -City 
Leading Index remained broadly flat, 
while the Hang Seng Index was range-
bound at around 25,000. The positive 
wealth effect seen in 1H has faded as 
asset market performance remained 
muted. A strong HKD continued to 
encourage outbound tourism, evidenced 
by an increase in average daily resident 
departures from 6.9% yoy in Q2 to 10.9% 
in Q3.

However, GDP growth is expected to be 
buffered by improving net exports amid 
the ongoing AI -electronics upcycle. 
Export growth picked up and outpaced 
import growth during Q3. Hong Kong, as 
China’s free port and re -export hub, is 
benefiting from stronger de mand for 
Chinese electronic goods.

Indonesia/ Philippines

### Page 4

The Week Ahead October 30, 2026 
                                                                                                                                         4

Indonesia’s 3Q GDP likely moderated to 
5.2% y/y. Soft consumer confidence and 
retail sales growth point to weaker 
consumption, compounded by a gradual 
rise in inflation. Fiscal measures likely 
helped spending among low -income 
households. Despite the quarte rly 
moderation, we maintain our full -year 
growth forecast at 5.3% y/y.

Inflation rates in Indonesia and the 
Philippines are expected to rise, albeit to 
different degrees. Indonesia’s inflation is 
on course to edge up modestly, while in 
the Philippines, stronger pass -through 
from higher oil prices, food and 
transportation costs, as well as imported 
inflation pressures stemming from a 
weaker peso, is likely to push inflation to 
a multi-year high.

## Document 447: US Equity Views_ Over-earning, but not an earnings bubble

Source PDF: all report pdf/US Equity Views_ Over-earning, but not an earnings bubble.pdf
Report source: Goldman Sachs Publishing
Date: 2026-09-17
Pages: 18
Extraction status: ok

### Page 2

semiconductor share prices have recently lagged the trajectory of near-term EPS
estimates. Similarly, the market trades at a P/E discount relative to what a macro model
would imply given the current level of corporate profitability. Our 12-month S&P 500
return forecast of +14% (to 8,700) reflects the view that earnings growth, rather than
expanding valuations, will remain the primary driver of the bull market.
17 September 2026   2
Goldman Sachs US Equity Views

### Page 3

The magnitude of S&P 500 “over-earning”

S&P 500 earnings grew by an extraordinary 51% year/year in Q2 2026 and have 
grown by 26% during the past four quarters. During the last 30 years, the recent pace 
of earnings growth has been exceeded only during the post-recession rebounds in 2010 
and 2021.

The recent strength of S&P 500 earnings growth has raised investor concerns that 
current earnings power is unsustainable and that the market is in an “earnings 
bubble.” S&P 500 earnings have recently outpaced both their historical trend and their 
historical relationship with US GDP growth. S&P 500 4-quarter EPS growth has averaged 
7% during the past 30 years, and the relationship between S&P 500 earnings and US 
economic growth in recent decades would have pointed to a similar rate of earnings 
growth during the last few quarters.

Exhibit 1: S&P 500 trailing 4-quarter EPS has grown by 26% during the past year

### Page 4

The degree to which the US equity market is currently “expensive” depends in large 
part on the sustainability of recent earnings strength. The exceptional recent 
strength of earnings has driven an unusual wedge between equity valuation multiples 
based on near-term earnings and valuations on trend earnings. Because equity prices 
have failed to keep pace with surging earnings, near-term valuations show no hint of a 
bubble: The S&P 500 12-month P/E ratio has declined from 23x a year ago to 19x today 
and now matches its 10-year average. However, even an “average” multiple may be 
expensive if current earnings are unsustainable. The cyclically-adjusted P/E based on 
trailing 10-year earnings has climbed to one of its highest levels on record, falling shy of 
the highs of 1999-2000 but surpassing the level reached in 2021. Similarly, our 
colleagues have argued that the increase in market value of AI companies during the last 
few years requires an optimistic estimate of the future magnitude of AI-related profits 
and who will capture them.

### Page 5

The AI boom and S&P 500 earnings growth

Affirming investor concerns that US companies are “over-earning,” there are three 
factors that are boosting earnings today but will contribute to deceleration going 
forward: 1) AI capex spending, 2) semiconductor margin expansion, and 3) earnings 
from private investment gains.

AI capex 
The AI investment boom is driving nearly half of S&P 500 EPS growth this year, but 
its contribution will fade going forward. The mega-cap US hyperscalers are on track to 
spend $800 billion on capex this year, an increase of 94% vs. 2025. That spending is 
flowing through to the earnings of the AI infrastructure complex including 
semiconductor, tech hardware, industrials, and utilities companies, which are collectively 
accounting for roughly half of consensus S&P 500 earnings growth this year. The AI 
boom is also having a secondary impact on earnings outside of the infrastructure 
complex, for example by boosting capital markets activity and consumer wealth. Both 
consensus and GS analyst forecasts show hyperscaler capex growing at a slower rate in 
coming years, which should result in decelerating earnings growth for much of the AI 
infrastructure complex and a fading tailwind to S&P 500 earnings.

### Page 6

Hyperscaler depreciation expenses will continue to increase as capex growth 
decelerates, further dampening the boost of AI investment spending to S&P 500 
earnings growth. We estimate a drag from hyperscaler depreciation expenses on S&P 
500 earnings growth of 5pp in 2027, offsetting nearly half of the 11pp boost to earnings 
from capex spending. By 2028, the drag from depreciation should offset the S&P 500 
earnings uplift from continued capex spending.

### Page 7

If capex were fully expensed, consensus 2027 S&P 500 EPS would be 22% lower than 
under current accounting rules. The current gap between actual reported and 
hypothetical fully-expensed earnings is one of the widest in recent decades, exceeded 
only as earnings fell more quickly than capex during the recessions of 2001 and 2008.

Hyperscaler capex has consistently surprised relative to consensus estimates during 
the last few years, and the potential for additional surprises going forward creates a 
wide range of potential S&P 500 earnings outcomes. Our equity analysts expect 
hyperscaler capex will total $1.2 trillion in 2027 and $1.4 trillion in 2028, in which case 
the current tailwind from AI capex to S&P 500 EPS will fade by 2028. Exhibit 10 below 
outlines upside and downside scenarios around that base case. Next year, a $250 billion 
surprise in hyperscaler capex — in either direction — would shift S&P 500 EPS growth by 
about 6 pp in the same direction.  
We estimate that hyperscaler capex would need to decline by roughly 30%, to $570 
billion, to offset earnings growth for the rest of the S&P 500 in 2027. This decline 
would reduce earnings for the AI infrastructure complex by about 40%, leaving their 
profits about 25% above 2025 levels.

### Page 8

Semiconductor margin expansion 
A sharp increase in profit margins for many semiconductor companies has 
contributed to the above-trend recent pace of S&P 500 earnings growth. The 
combination of strong demand and limited supply has boosted semiconductor prices 
and gross margins, with the most pronounced margin expansion among memory stocks. 
S&P 500 memory firms currently enjoy gross margins of roughly 80%, more than double 
their historical average. Across the semiconductor industry, gross margins sit at their 
highest level in decades, and consensus expects margins to remain at similar levels 
through 2028.

Margin expansion has accounted for a large share of recent semiconductor earnings 
growth, but that boost should fade going forward. We estimate that about 1/4 of S&P 
500 Semiconductor earnings growth in 2026 is being driven by gross margin expansion. 
Our equity analysts believe the balance of memory supply and demand is likely to 
remain tight through 2027, with incremental supply additions unlikely to impact industry 
margins until 2028 at the earliest. However, they expect the rate of incremental margin 
expansion to slow meaningfully next year, reducing the contribution of semiconductors

S&P 500 earnings growth in the next few years will be highly sensitive to the 
trajectory of semiconductor margins. In a scenario where slowing AI infrastructure 
investment, increasing supply, and/or technological shift lowers semiconductor prices 
and profit margins, S&P 500 EPS growth would also disappoint. We estimate that each 
percentage point change in S&P 500 semiconductor gross margins next year would shift 
S&P 500 EPS growth by about 1 pp.

### Page 10

“Other income” from private investment gains 
Appreciating equity investment stakes are also temporarily inflating S&P 500 
earnings. Mega-cap tech earnings have recently been boosted by large GAAP “other 
income” related to their equity investments. In Q2 2026, the mega-cap tech companies 
enjoyed unrealized investment gains in private companies that totaled roughly $150 
billion, translating into 12% of S&P 500 EPS. We expect H2 2026 reports will include 
additional “other income” that should be larger than the gains in Q1 but smaller than Q2 
totals. We expect a much smaller contribution in 2027. The complete removal of this 
“other income” next year would create a drag of 8 pp on S&P 500 earnings growth in 
2027 relative to 2026, all else equal.

### Page 11

S&P 500 earnings outlook

Our baseline forecast is for S&P 500 EPS to rise to $415 in 2027 (+11% year/year) 
and to $460 in 2028 (+11%). We expect an above-consensus base of earnings in 2026 
due to further “other income” gains from private equity stakes in H2 of this year. 
Excluding the “other income” inflating earnings in 2026, S&P 500 earnings growth next 
year would register 18%. Our 2027 EPS forecast is similar to the bottom-up consensus 
estimate of $419 but above the median top-down strategist estimate of $403.

### Page 12

Our 2028 EPS forecast is about 5% below the bottom-up consensus, signaling a 
return to the typical historical pattern of downward revisions to consensus earnings 
estimates after the last two years of rising estimates. Outside of the complex of AI 
capex beneficiaries, we expect sales growth will register close to nominal GDP , margins 
will expand modestly, and a growing boost from AI productivity will result in earnings 
growth of 9% in both 2027 and 2028.  Our forecasts for EPS growth among this group of 
companies are roughly 4-5% below consensus estimates in both 2027 and 2028, similar 
to the historical average magnitude of downward revision to consensus estimates. While 
AI investment spending has had a direct impact on the earnings of semiconductor and 
other AI infrastructure stocks, it has also lifted earnings for companies outside of the “ AI 
complex,” including by boosting capital markets activity as well as supporting consumer 
spending via the wealth effect. 
Solid GDP growth and easing pressure from energy prices will be the main macro 
tailwinds for earnings growth in 2027. Economic growth is the primary variable in our 
top-down earnings model and explains more than half of the historical variation in S&P 
500 EPS growth. Our economists expect US real GDP growth will average 2.1% in 2027 
and 2.3% in 2028. Our commodity strategists expect Brent crude oil will decline to 
$80/bbl by the end of 2027, which would weigh modestly on Energy sector profits but 
boost earnings for most other sectors. Outside of the AI-related risks outlined above, 
persistent input cost pressures and risks to the consumer from equity market volatility 
are two of the key macro downside risks to our earnings forecast.

### Page 13

As the earnings tailwind from AI investment spending fades, the trajectory of S&P 
500 profitability in coming years will depend increasingly on the realization of AI 
productivity gains. Our forecasts embed a 1 pp boost to S&P 500 EPS growth from AI 
productivity in 2027 and a 2 pp boost in 2028. Economy-wide surveys and corporate 
commentary signal enterprise adoption that is increasing but remains in early stages, 
and the impact of AI adoption on corporate earnings still appears narrow. However, the 
recent acceleration in enterprise AI spending suggests that the earnings impact of AI 
adoption should become clearer in coming quarters.

### Page 14

Valuing S&P 500 earnings

Like our forecasts and consensus estimates, market pricing suggests investors 
anticipate a deceleration in earnings growth going forward. This is true both for the 
broad market and for AI infrastructure stocks. For example, the share prices of 
semiconductor stocks have historically mirrored the path of consensus EPS estimates, 
and the recent moderation of semiconductor returns has aligned with consensus 
estimates for decelerating growth in 2027 and 2028.

The current S&P 500 P/E multiple signals an optimistic outlook but market 
skepticism regarding the sustainability of current profits. In addition to rising interest 
rates, an anticipated deceleration in earnings helps explain the recent decline in the S&P 
500 forward P/E multiple. Along with macro factors like interest rates, the level of 
corporate profitability has helped explain the variation in S&P 500 valuation over time. 
Based on the historical relationship between S&P 500 ROE and P/E, the current S&P 500 
ROE of 24% — a record high — would point to a forward 12-month P/E of over 21x 
against today’s macro backdrop. Given current inflation and interest rates, today’s S&P 
500 multiple of 19x would be consistent with an ROE of roughly 22%, more than 200 bp 
below the current level but otherwise matching 2021 as the highest on record.

### Page 15

Earnings, rather than valuations, should continue to drive equity market upside 
from here. Our 3-, 6-, and 12-month S&P 500 return targets are +5 (8000), +9% (8300), 
and +14% (8700). These imply a P/E multiple on consensus forward EPS that remains 
close to the current level of 19x. Our economists and interest rate strategists expect 
interest rates to decline modestly in coming quarters, creating some macro upside risk to 
equity valuations. However, we expect investor uncertainty regarding the impact of AI 
on the outlook for long-term profits to persist in coming quarters, making a dramatic 
increase in valuations unlikely.

## Document 448: US MARKETS CALL_ Still Proceeding Cautiously

Source PDF: all report pdf/US MARKETS CALL_ Still Proceeding Cautiously.pdf
Report source: researchmail轉寄
Date: 2026-10-24
Pages: 8
Extraction status: ok

### Page 1

From:    Yardeni QuickTakes <yardeni-research@ghost.io>
Subject: US MARKETS CALL: Still Proceeding Cautiously
Date:    3 hours ago
US MARKETS CALL: Still Proceeding Cautiously

YARDENI QUICKTAKES
US MARKETS CALL: Still
Proceeding Cautiously
By Ed Yardeni & Toby Hearst • 24 Oct 2026
View in browser
Photo by Jordan Christian / Unsplash

On September 15, we advised investors to proceed with caution. We
pushed out our 8,400 S&P 500 target from year-end 2026 to mid-2027
and set 7,900 as our new year-end target. On October 14, we reiterated
our caution. Sure enough, the stock market has been turbulent since mid-
September, with AI-related stocks falling sharply.

### Page 2

We are seeing some buying opportunities, but better ones are likely after
the November 3 midterm elections. Our main concern is a likely escalation
of the Middle East war. That could push oil prices above their $120 peak
earlier this year.
I. Stock Market Performance
The S&P 500 is still up double digits ytd at 11.4% (chart). XMAGS is up
13.1%, while MAGS is up only 6.2%. The gap has widened again in recent
weeks as MAGS rolled over. Meta led the latest leg down, falling 9.4% last

week after reporting earnings.
Breadth remained on the weak side. Only 37.8% of S&P 500 companies
are outperforming the index on a y/y basis (chart).

### Page 3

Rotation was notable last week. The S&P 500 Energy and Health Care
sectors led with gains of 1.5% each (chart). Communication Services fell
6.2%, and Industrials dropped 1.9%. As bad as the week felt, the S&P 500
was down only 0.5%.
II. S&P 500 Earnings
S&P 500 forward earnings per share rose to a record $414.41 during the
week of October 22 (chart). It is converging on the analysts’ 2027
consensus estimate of $424.56, up from this year's estimate of $365.94

(chart).
The 2026 consensus is closing in on our $375 per-share forecast (chart).
The consensus for 2027 matches our $425 forecast. The 2028 consensus
is already $493.32, well above our $450 forecast for that year and almost

### Page 4

at our $500 target for 2030! If the analysts are right, the S&P 500 could
rise to 10,000 by the end of next year. We are sticking with that target for
the end of 2029. We think the analysts are too bullish on earnings.
Forward earnings for the S&P 400 and S&P 600 are also at record highs
(chart).
III. Valuation
Valuation multiples continue to compress despite strong earnings (chart).
The Magnificent-7's forward P/E is 22.8, down from a 2026 peak of 28.5.

The S&P 500, S&P 400, and S&P 600 trade at 18.4, 14.5 and 14.0,
respectively.

### Page 5

The big valuation story is that for the S&P 500 Information Technology
sector. Its forward P/E dropped from 26.5 at the end of 2025 to 19.0 now
(chart). The S&P 500 has fallen from 22.2 to 18.6.
The S&P 500's forward earnings is up 31.0% ytd (chart). The index is up
11.4%. The forward P/E is down 13.5%. Stocks have gotten significantly
cheaper this year.

### Page 6

IV. Bonds
The 10-year Treasury yield is back in the “old normal” range of
4.00%-5.00% (chart). It peaked at 5.35%, attracting many buyers. It is
now at 4.96%. Some of that decline reflects safe-haven buying during the
recent risk-off selloff in the stock market. But market-based measures of
long-term inflation expectations remain subdued. Bond investors seem to
expect inflation to subside. We agree.
The 13-week change in the 10-year yield tends to track the Citigroup

Economic Surprise Index (CESI) (chart). Recently, the yield's change
overshot the index, as it has done before. Now, the 10-year yield is up
27bps over the past 13 weeks, down from over 80bps a few weeks ago.

### Page 7

Corporate credit default swap spreads are breaking out again (chart). The
widening reflects credit-quality concerns about companies like Oracle and
CoreWeave. The Bond Vigilantes are regulating credit in the AI
ecosystem. That's healthy. We aren't particularly concerned.
💡

log in to the Yardeni QuickTakes website and post them at the end
of the QuickTakes article. Paid members' contributions may be

featured in our segment, "Ed Answers Your Questions".

中国：綱紀粛正強化と経済の萎縮

[要約] 
 中国共産党第20 期中央委員会第5 回全体会議（五中全会）が、2026 年10 月26 日から
29 日にかけて北京で開催され、「中国共産党が全面的かつ厳格な統治を持続的に推進
するためのいくつかの重大問題に関する決定」を採択した。中国では汚職・腐敗撲滅、
綱紀粛正の嵐が吹いている。 
 今後、体制の引き締めが維持・強化される見通しであり、経済活動が一部萎縮するリ
スクに注意する必要がある。汚職・腐敗撲滅や綱紀粛正が強化される中、習近平総書
記への忠誠を高めることが最も重要なポイントとなる。この点で、習近平氏の関心が
どこに向かうかが決定的に重要だが、関心は経済よりも政治に向かいやすい。 「景気テ
コ入れ」と「綱紀粛正」の指示が同時に出れば、 「綱紀粛正」の影響が勝ることになろ
う。こうした状況下では、悪目立ちをしないでやり過ごす、 「不作為」という状況に陥
りやすくなる。大和総研では、2027 年の成長率見通しを前年比 4.4％と、2026 年の同
4.6％から若干減速するとみているのは、この影響を懸念しているからだ。

### Page 2

2 / 3

汚職・腐敗撲滅、綱紀粛正の嵐 
中国共産党第20 期中央委員会第5 回全体会議（五中全会）が、2026 年 10 月 26 日から29 日
にかけて北京で開催され、 「中国共産党が全面的かつ厳格な統治を持続的に推進するためのい
くつかの重大問題に関する決定」 （以下、決定）を採択して閉幕した。決定の中で印象的だっ
たのは「不敢腐、不能腐、不想腐」 （腐敗させず、腐敗できず、腐敗したいと思わない）とい
うフレーズだ。腐敗・汚職撲滅と綱紀粛正の継続・強化が随所で言及されている。 
中国では汚職・腐敗撲滅、綱紀粛正の嵐が吹いている。五中全会に参加した中央委員は 162
名と発表された。2022 年 10 月に習近平政権 3 期目がスタートした当時は 205 名であり、それ
との比較では 21.0％の減少だ。ただし、この間に中央委員会候補委員から中央委員に昇格した
人々1がいることを考えると、減少の延べ人数はもっと多いことになる。全体の2 割だとしても
異常な多さであり、汚職・腐敗撲滅、綱紀粛正の凄まじさを物語っている。こうした状況下で
習近平氏の任命責任が厳しく問われてもおかしくないが、そのような声は全く聞かれない。 
今回の五中全会では、馬興瑞・元政治局委員、張又侠・元政治局委員（元党中央軍事委員会
副主席）ら 33 名の党籍剥奪処分を改めて確認した。うち 16 名が軍部であった。党中央軍事委
員会は習近平政権の 3 期目発足時点では 7 名体制でスタートしたが、失脚に次ぐ失脚で、2026
年 1 月以降は習近平主席と張昇民副主席の2 名体制となっていた（図表1） 。五中全会では、張
昇民副主席の政治局委員への昇格が発表されたが、党軍事委員会の補充は行われなかった。指
揮系統の中枢で5 名分の空席が補充されない異常事態が続いている。

図表 1 党中央軍事委員会メンバー（2022 年 10 月の発足当時からの変化）

「不作為」による経済の萎縮リスク 
五中全会では、中国共産党第 21 回全国代表大会を 2027 年下半期に北京で開催することが発
表された（通常は、10 月か 11 月に開催される） 。これは5 年に一度開催される最重要会議であ
り、直後の第21 期中央委員会第1 回全体会議（一中全会）で今後5 年間の新指導体制が決定さ
れる。現状では、習近平総書記の4 期目突入がほぼ確実視され、その後継候補となり得る1970

### Page 3

1

3 / 3

年代生まれの人物が、中枢である中央政治局常務委員に抜擢されるかが焦点になっている。 
経済との関係でいえば、党大会が開催される西暦下一桁が2 か 7 の年は、前年よりも成長率
が加速することが多かった（図表 2） 。秋に党大会が開催され、翌年3 月の全国人民代表大会
（全人代）では、国家機構の人事が決定される。中国共産党・政府関係者にとって、5 年に一度
の昇進の大チャンスが訪れることになる。自分が担当する地域や分野のパフォーマンスを向上
させることが出世の早道と目されたのである。 
しかし、今後、体制の引き締めが維持・強化される見通しであり、経済活動が一部萎縮する
リスクに注意する必要がある。汚職・腐敗撲滅や綱紀粛正が強化される中、習近平総書記への
忠誠を高めることが最も重要なポイントとなる。この点で、習近平氏の関心がどこに向かうか
が決定的に重要だ。結論を先にいえば、関心は経済よりも政治に向かいやすい。 「景気テコ入
れ」と「綱紀粛正」の指示が同時に出れば、 「綱紀粛正」の影響が勝ることになろう。こうし
た状況下では、悪目立ちをしないでやり過ごす、 「不作為」という状況に陥りやすくなる。大
和総研では、2027 年の成長率見通しを前年比 4.4％と、2026 年の同 4.6％から若干減速すると
みているのは、この影響を懸念しているからだ。

図表 2 中国共産党大会開催年を基準とした実質 GDP 成長率の推移（単位：％）

-2年 7.8 13.4 3.9 11.0  8.6  11.5  10.6  7.0  2.3  5.0

### Page 3

SK海力士：2029年的产能已售罄，短缺持续至2030年
10月28日，SK海力士在2026年三季度业绩发布会上交出了一份历史级成绩单——单季营收首次突破100万亿
韩元，营业利润率攀升至80%的历史峰值。
管理层在电话会上明确表示：结合长期供应协议（LTA）锁定量和正在洽谈中的客户需求，“我们DRAM和
HBM未来三年（截至2029年）的产能实际上已全部售罄“。
需求如此旺盛，客户的采购需求量超出我们当前供应能力的两倍以上。
2027年DRAM市场可实现需求增速约20%中段（mid 20%），NAND约10%高段（high 10%）。
管理层同时强调供给端约束的结构性："由于先进制程实施难度不断上升，以及新建产能所需的建设周期，供
不应求的格局预计将持续至2030年“。
全球AI数据中心基础设施市场预计到2030年保持40%的年均增长率。
公司的商业模式正在从传统的“周期性波动”转向基于长期协议的“高可见度确定性增长”。

数据来源：SK海力士，格林大华期货

### Page 4

三星：2028年很难有新增的存储实际产能
10月29日的三季度业绩电话会：供给紧张将延续至2028年。
2027年CapEx增量的相当一部分将用于购置新场地，而非直接用于产能扩张；其次，建设成本上升使得
CapEx数字看上去比实际更大；再者，从新建晶圆厂到实际出片存在较长的建设周期，2027年的投资很难在
2028年形成有效bit产出。
设备价格因供给紧张而上涨，叠加预付款锁产要求，使资本开支数字进一步虚高；设备交期拉长也会制约存
储厂商的产能爬坡节奏。
随着HBM4E和HBM5逐步成为主流产品，其die尺寸将大于上一代，金属制程层数也将显著增加；同时，常规
DRAM向HBM的生产转移将构成物理性供给约束，"这是不可避免的"。
2027年HBM供货量已按目标价格售罄，但仍持续收到大客户的额外需求请求。
三季度，三星已收到大批量QLC SSD追加订单，这一趋势与云端个人AI Agent服务的爆发直接相关。
三星已与10家主要客户完成多年期供应合同签署，另有10家主要客户的谈判正在进行，其中多家已接近最终

阶段。                                                                         数据来源：三星，格林大华期货

### Page 5

闪迪：客户大举追加长单，NBM订单已排至2030年；高带宽闪存（HBF）存储芯片已完成流片
10月30日的2027财年第一季度财报，公司当季实现营收110亿美元，环比增长23%，同比暴增378%；非
GAAP毛利率达到85.1%。
本季度营收增长中“约60%归功于销量增长，40%归功于价格上涨。”基于强劲的订单可见度，闪迪预计第
二财季营收将在122亿至126亿美元之间。公司承诺将100%的盈余现金返还给股东。
随着AI大模型的演进，推理工作负载正在发生结构性变化。更长的提示、更复杂的推理、多模态输入和持久
的用户上下文都会显著增加存储需求。闪存则提供高效保留更广泛上下文所需的容量，避免高昂的重新计算。
从客户那里了解到，他们低估了运行基础设施所需的NAND闪存容量。因此，我们有很多客户再次订购，同
时也在努力拓展新客户。目前不仅有很多老客户在追加订单，新客户也在不断涌入。
闪迪2027年约50%的供应、2028年约三分之二的供应均已被NBM项目预订。
首款高带宽闪存（HBF）存储芯片已完成流片。Goeckeler解释称：“HBF是一种用于推理的新型存储架构，

其读取带宽与HBM相当，但容量更大……旨在通过将NAND级容量更靠近处理器来补充HBM。” 预计2027
年将向客户交付首批样品。
数据来源：闪迪，格林大华期货

### Page 6

微软云Azure本季度收入增速达48%，预计下季度资本开支将超过500亿美元
美东时间10月28日，微软云Azure本季度收入增速达48%，高于市场预期，下季度指引区间进一步提升至
49%至50%的固定汇率增速。
本季度Azure强劲表现背后，效率改善是关键变量。工程团队在GPU和CPU层面持续挖掘效率空间，使微软得
以将原本受供给约束的算力更多分配至Foundry平台。
本季度token定价高于预期，高质量模型带来了更高的每token收益，对季度业绩形成额外贡献。
客户的需求继续超过了我们可用的供应。
企业客户的AI部署正从试验阶段转向生产部署，关注点已从"下一个技术亮点"转向"能否构建稳定系统、掌控
模型权重、管控成本和保障安全"。
企业级业务不是每个季度看一次的东西。这是一个长达十年的过程。
本季度资本支出达452亿美元，部分第三方算力交付延迟至下半年。预计下季度资本开支将超过500亿美元，
全年合计约1700亿美元，下半年将进一步加速。
数据来源：微软，格林大华期货

### Page 7

谷歌电话会：云收入劲增103%，云业务积压订单高达5610亿美元，
10月28日谷歌电话会：云收入劲增103%。
云业务积压订单高达5610亿美元，TPU收入大规模确认将在2027年到来。
就像十年前我们转向移动端一样，我们专注于先打造最佳搜索用户体验，然后再实现变现。
自由现金流转负主要受到TPU库存前置的“时间差”影响，TPU收入将在2027年放量，2027年是收获之年。
由于供给受限，谷歌已在Q3末开始启用第三方计算容量，Q4将进一步加大使用。
TPU系统不再只在谷歌云上提供，现在已可部署在客户自己的数据中心。
2027年资本开支也将“显著增加”。谷歌母公司Alphabet预计全年资本开支1950亿-2050亿美元。
在整个投资周期中，我们预计将产生有吸引力的投入资本回报率，且将显著高于我们的资本成本，并预计将
产生强劲的运营现金流。
数据来源：谷歌，格林大华期货

亚马逊电话会：云收入劲增103%，云业务积压订单高达5610亿美元，
10月29日亚马逊电话会：云服务部门AWS第三季度营收达到459.84亿美元，较上年同期的330.06亿美元增
长约39%，创近五年来最快同比增速，年化收入运行规模达到约1840亿美元。AWS营业利润达到177.73亿美
元，较上年同期的114.34亿美元增长约55%。
AWS加速发展，公司预计2027年和2028年的收入增长率将“超过40%”。

Trainium芯片。

美元。过去12个月，公司购置物业和设备净额达到2106.6亿美元，同比增长82%。
亚马逊预计今年的支出仍将达到2200亿美元。公司预计“2027年将大幅增加支出”，并且亚马逊“有望获得
可观的财务回报”。

产生强劲的运营现金流。                                                                          数据来源：亚马逊，格林大华期货

### Page 14

CoreWeave债券波动不会放缓AI基建增速
数据来源：CoreWeave，格林大华期货
CoreWeave是新兴云服务商，缺乏传统业务现金流支持，主要依靠发债募资，其本次2031年到期的债券收益
率达到15%，发行时债券收益率即高达9.6%，票面利率为9%。
算力租赁价格在继续上升，云服务商的算力服务盈利极为强劲，当前算力基础设施投入一年即可收回成本。
市场担忧的是CoreWeave从算力基础设施投入到获得算力收入，用时过长，现金流能否覆盖利息与资本开支。
划重点：100%的利润  VS 15%的融资成本，哪个更高呢？
CoreWeave公司CEO 表示，称公司拥有逾1042亿美元的订单积压，完全足以支撑所有债务义务。
英伟达CEO黄仁勋出面“灭火”，称CoreWeave财务实力持续改善、融资成本正在下降。一锤定音。
CoreWeave联合创始人Brannin McBee日前接受《金融时报》采访时表示，公司希望获得投资级信用评级，
从而打开更广泛的融资渠道，甚至能够借入“无限”规模的资金池。

退一步讲，即使CoreWeave无法再融资，也只是拱手将快速增长的算力市场让给其它云服务商，而并非意味
着算力市场会出现衰退。单家公司的信用风险和指数级增长的算力需求是两个不同概念。AI基建不会放缓。

### Page 15

10月14日台积电：需求极其强劲，扩产速度前所未有，全球目前有近25项在建项目
数据来源：美光，格林大华期货
2026 Q3业绩电话会：第三季度净利润同比增幅达69%。预计2026年全年的营收将同比增长约44%。
台积电预计第四季度销售额区间为523亿至543亿美元，以中间值533亿美元计算，较市场此前预估的503.5亿
美元高出约6%，显示需求势头依然强劲。
台积电CEO：需求极其强劲，我们没有看到任何放缓迹象。复合年增长率是否处于百分之五十几的高段，并
不是重点，实际增长可能会更高。
台积电确认2026年资本支出区间为600亿至640亿美元，创历史新高。市场对台积电2027年资本支出的一致
预期持续上调，已成为半导体行业景气度的重要前瞻指标。高盛目前预测该数字为850亿美元，瑞银预测为
900亿美元。
台积电正以前所未有的速度扩张，全球目前有近25项在建项目。未来几年台积电将在中国台湾建设13座先进
制程及先进封装工厂，并在海外建设另外5至6座工厂。台积电宣布在台南再增建一座3纳米晶圆厂。

AI加速器强劲的基础需求持续支持AI相关业务。同时，面向智能体AI的CPU需求兴起，以及网络芯片需求持
续增长，也在进一步推动芯片需求。

阿斯麦CEO：设备订单已经排到 2028年，不少企业已经提前洽谈 2029 乃至 2030 年光刻机采购
数据来源：ASML，格林大华期货

### Page 17

美光电话会： 2027年产能已售75%  看不到供需平衡拐点
数据来源：美光，格林大华期货
美光科技公布截至9月3日的2026财年第四财季的业绩报告。数据显示，美光Q4单季营收达到创纪录的542亿
美元，环比增长31%，同比狂飙379%；单季毛利率大幅攀升至87%。
美光目前已签署26项长协锁定约1500亿美元长期订单，2027和2028年存储市场供需将比2026年更为紧缺，
且“看不到供需恢复平衡的尽头”。
结构性的供需增长率差距导致了持续的供应紧张。即使考虑到行业新增的DRAM洁净室空间计划，在强劲的
需求趋势（包括客户不断提出的增量要求）下，我们看不到供需何时能恢复平衡的拐点。
美光2027年超过75%的产出已经获得客户承诺，且当下与客户的大多数谈判已经围绕2028年展开。
在26份SCA及延期协议中，客户的资金承诺已增加至320亿美元，绝大多数为现金存款。客户希望获得2030
年以后的供应保障，我们现在已经签署了延伸至2031年的SCA。
Q4单季，美光产生了惊人的440亿美元运营现金流和332亿美元自由现金流。预计将把100%的超额现金返还

给股东。我们打算从2026年12月9日开始增加资本返还，主要通过股票回购来实现。
公司预计2027财年上半年的CapEx约为250亿美元，下半年将更高，且大部分增量将用于新建晶圆厂（建筑
支出）而非单纯的设备采购。

### Page 18

市场开始押注：面向普通消费者的智能体，可能正在接近大规模普及的转折点
数据来源：Meta，格林大华期货
Muse 的登顶则表明，个人 Agent 正在从产业概念真正走进大众消费市场。这是一个非常强的早期信号，意
味着大家愿意把真实的任务交给 AI 去办，有这个需求。
Muse 在云端给每个用户配了一台独立的虚拟机，里面有一个真正的浏览器，可以安全地存储 Agent 的数据
和已连接服务的凭证。
AI 在这台云端电脑里完成浏览网页、填写表单、调用服务等各类任务。当涉及发送邮件或完成付款等敏感操
作时，Muse 会先请求用户批准。
Agent 已经是共识，产业竞争的焦点是谁能控制 Agent 入口。
Meta 在旧模式还极其赚钱的时候，主动开启了可能颠覆自己旧模式的个人 Agent 业务。Meta 认定一场产
业革命要来，就主动革了自己的命。
Meta 手里握着超 30 亿的日活用户，一旦通过这个基本盘占住了个人 Agent 这个新入口，也就将成为
Agent 时代最强的玩家之一。
超级 App 未必会消失，但它们将逐渐从第一入口，变成 Agent 背后的服务提供者。

### Page 19

GPU云服务掀涨价潮   Nebius再提价20%   客户预订2028年一季度和二季度的算力
数据来源：Nebius，格林大华期货
Nebius宣布于10月1日起全面上调旗下GPU云服务定价，平均涨幅约20%。
此轮提价覆盖H100、H200、B200及B300多款芯片型号，是Nebius今年5月以来的第二轮涨价。
市场将此解读为AI算力需求持续过热、供给仍然紧张的强烈信号。
H100每GPU小时价格从3.85美元升至4.50美元，涨幅约16.9%；
H200每GPU小时价格从4.50美元升至5.40美元，涨幅约 20%；
B200每GPU小时价格从7.15美元升至8.50美元，涨幅约18.9%；
B300每GPU小时价格从7.85美元升至9.50美元，涨幅约 21%；
以B300为例，其价格从5月前的约6.10美元/小时计算，至今累计涨幅已达约56%。
Nebius管理层透露，已有客户预订2028年一季度和二季度的算力，部分订单涉及数万块GPU，公司当前的需
求能见度已超过24个月，较此前约18个月明显拉长。

推算Nebius当前短期客户合同价格可能已达每兆瓦6000万美元以上，未来Vera Rubin架构产品的合同价格或
将突破每兆瓦8000万美元。

### Page 20

OpenAI重磅官宣下一代旗舰模型GPT-6 Astra，欢迎人类进入AGI时代
数据来源：OpenAI，格林大华期货
9月4日，OpenAI重磅官宣下一代旗舰模型GPT-6。它在计算机使用、浏览、软件工程、网络安全、科学和专
业工作领域，全部刷新SOTA。
总裁Greg Brockman宣布：OpenAI已经实现了AGI！Welcome to the AGI era（欢迎来到AGI时代）！在
我看来，世界正式步入AGI时代，也就是AI的综合智力彻底碾压人类了。
计算机使用代际飞跃：Astra可以完成你在电脑上能做的任何事，速度飞快。
在各项基准测试中，OpenAI官博中多次重用了一个词——饱和（saturate），各项天花板级基准彻底被打爆，
榜单已测不出它的上限了，显示GPT-6 Astra在多个领域的绝对统治力。
AI亲自训练AI：这是OpenAI首款在「训练监督」中由先前AI模型发挥重要作用的模型。
OpenAI把Astra称为，世界上最好的计算机使用模型。奥特曼之前反复强调，下一代最让他兴奋的，就是这

一功能。他直言：Astra计算机使用能力，已达人类水平。
GPT-6 Astra已彻底进化为一个坐在电脑前、手握键鼠的超级专家。

### Page 21

SemiAnalysis创始人：算力收益狂飙，两大巨头将垄断全球绝大部分可用算力，推升全球发债利率
数据来源：SemiAnalysis，格林大华期货
SemiAnalysis创始人Dylan Patel预计，2028年OpenAI与Anthropic两大巨头将垄断全球绝大部分可用算力。
今年新增上线算力中约三分之一被 OpenAI 和 Anthropic 瓜分，到 2028 年，两家头部实验室预计将拿下全
球 70%至80% 的增量算力，单家算力规模将超过 50 吉瓦（GW）。更为关键的是性能乘数效应：今年部署
的每一瓦特算力，其搭载的 GB300、TPUv7 或 Trainium3，在每瓦性能上比上一代芯片高出 3 到 5 倍。当
这两家实验室吃下 2028 年全球 70%至80% 的新增算力时，由于这批算力具有最高的能效比，它们实际上垄
断了全世界绝大部分可用的有效计算能力。
算力收益狂飙：算力的基础成本通常在 1000 万至 1500 万美元/兆瓦，而Anthropic 的算力产出已达每兆瓦

5000 万美元，全公司综合收入向 7000 万至 8000 万美元/兆瓦进发，并正向 1 亿美元迈进，这支撑它们以
每兆瓦 2500 万至 5000 万美元的天价向市场扫货。
AI算力的惊人暴利正掀起投资狂潮。2024 至 2029 年全球 AI 相关资本开支预计达 11 万亿美元，其中需通过
信贷市场举债超 5 万亿美元。
由于 AI 数据中心展现出惊人的投资回报率，科技巨头愿意承受 8% 甚至更高的发债成本，对资本的极端争夺
将推高全社会的折现率与发债利率，甚至引发全球借贷成本的系统性上升。
科技巨头的天价举债推高全社会借贷利率，不仅将重创传统资产估值，更将引爆新一轮主权债务危机。

史纪录97.5%，间接投标人获配80.3%、为历史第四高，显示外资需求异常强劲。5%收益率已具有极

数据来源：文华财经，格林大华期货

### Page 25

全球最大资管贝莱德转向 看多短期美债
全球最大资产管理公司贝莱德（BlackRock）宣布对美国国债和股票的核心配置立场作出重大调整，标志着其
坚守近六年的低配美债策略正式终结。
贝莱德投资研究院将短期美国国债上调至超配，将长期美债由低配上调至中性，理由是当前收益率水平已具
吸引力，且市场对美联储加息的预期可能“过度”。
贝莱德策略师：如今收益率已回归我们长期以来预判的高利率水平，希望锁定部分具有吸引力的收益。
贝莱德团队表示，在当前收益率水平下，即便未来12个月内收益率再上行75个基点，美债所产生的利息收入
仍足以抵消价格损失，总回报不至于转负。这一测算为其超配短期美债提供了量化支撑。
贝莱德认为市场当前对美联储加息的定价已走得过远。利率互换数据显示，交易员预计未来12个月内美联储
将至少加息三次，每次25个基点。贝莱德认为，一旦这些押注被修正，短期美债将尤为受益。
贝莱德此次转向，是大型资产管理机构在收益率大幅上行后集体重新审视固定收益配置的缩影。

数据来源：WIND，格林大华期货

一股反通胀的力量。
7、美国是超过30万亿美元的庞大经济体，货币政策和金融条件收紧正在给其他领域降温。

【全球经济展望】

至2030年。谷歌云第三季度云营收增长103%至307亿美元。微软云Azure本季度收入增速达48%，下季度指引区间
进一步提升至49%至50%的增速。甲骨文预计2028财年下半年自由现金流将转为正值，2029财年自由现金流将超过
300亿美元。SK海力士预计2027年DRAM市场需求将同比增长20%中段，NAND需求增速则在高十位数百分比区间，短
缺将持续至2030年。台积电管理层确认2026年资本支出600至640亿美元，并明确2026至2028年累计支出将大幅超
越过去三年约1010亿美元总量。阿斯麦CEO表示，设备订单已经排到 2028，不少企业已经提前洽谈 2029 乃至
2030 年光刻机采购。贝莱德近六年来首次终结低配美债立场，将短期美债升至超配——核心逻辑是10年期收益
率已从0.9%飙升至5.2%以上，即便未来再涨75个基点仍可正回报。美国企业今年已授权创纪录的1.3万亿美元回

购，执行窗口从10月15日起陆续重开。
全球处于加息周期，债市持续面临巨大压力，债市是大类资产中的金丝雀。

## Document 594: 内外経済ウォッチ『日本～米国の「リフレ脱却」要請と日米経済政策の実態～』(2026年11月号)

Source PDF: all report pdf/内外経済ウォッチ『日本～米国の「リフレ脱却」要請と日米経済政策の実態～』(2026年11月号).pdf
Report source: DLRI
Date: 2026-10-20
Pages: 1
Extraction status: ok

### Page 1

日本
～米国の 「リフレ脱却」 要請と日米経済政策の実態～
2026年9月、 米国のスコ ッ ト ・ ベッセン ト財務長官が日
本政府および日本銀行に対し、 「アベノミクス的リフレ政
策からの脱却」 および 「利上げ ・ 積極財政の抑制」 を要請し
た発言は、 日米の金融 ・ 財政当局に大きな波紋を広げた。
しかし、 日米両国の財政赤字 ・ 金利体系 ・ インフレ動向 ・
通貨価値の変遷を客観的に比較 ・ 分析すると、 「リフレ政
策」 を真に推進しているのは日本ではなく、 むしろ米国自
身であるという逆説的な構造が浮き彫りとなる。
2026年9月初旬、 ベッセン ト米財務長官はＧ20等の機
会を通じて 「日本はアベノミクス期の過度なリフレ政策を
終えるべきだ」 と指摘し、 日銀による利上げ推進や政府の
財政拡大抑制を遠回しに迫った。
米国が日本にリフレ脱却を求める表面的な理由は 「円
安是正とインフレ抑制」 だが、 実質的には日本の利回り上
昇に伴い、 日本勢の資金が米債市場から逃避し、 米長期

金利が跳ね上がる事態を恐れている側面を指摘する向
きもある。 しかし、 「骨太シ ョ ッ ク」 と言われた骨太原案が公
表された6月30日以降のG7諸国の長期金利の動向を確
認すると、 最も上昇幅が少ないのは日本となっている。
① 「財政赤字」 による需要刺激
米国はパンデミ ッ ク以降も、 IRA （インフレ削減法） や
CHIPS法、 そして減税策の恒久化などを背景に、 景気拡
大局面にもかかわらず対GDP比6％超という戦時並みの
財政赤字を続けている。 これこそがリフレ政策の本質で
ある 「政府が借金をして民間に需要を創出する仕組み」 そ
はじめに
ベッセン ト発言の背景と波紋
なぜ米国のほうが 「リフレ推進度合いが
強い」 と言えるのか ？
のものである。 一方、 日本の財政赤字／GDP比は2025
年時点でG7諸国最小水準となっている。
② 純債務残高／GDP比の悪化
米国の一般政府純債務残高対GDP比は100％に近づ
いており、 上昇 トレン ドとなっている。 AIブームと トランプ

減税が相まって、 国内の資金需給がひっ迫しており、 米長
期金利の上昇が構造的な財政悪化をもたら している。 対
して日本の一般政府純債務残高対GDP比は、 インフレ経
済が定着した2023年以降に急速に低下しており、 2025
年時点では80％を下回っている。
③日本の金融政策正常化との対比
日本側はアベノミクス期の異次元緩和から離脱し、 日
銀は政策金利を段階的に引き上げ、 保有国債残高も年間
40～50兆円ペースで縮小させている。 すなわち、 日本が
「リフレ出口」 に向かって足早に動いている一方で、 米国は
伝統的金融政策面では利上げスタンスにシフ ト しつつ、
量的金融政策面ではバランスシー トの縮小をス ト ップし
ているのが実態である。
ベッセン ト財務長官による日本への 「リフレをやめよ」 と
いう言明は、 自国の財政膨張が招いている米長期金利の
上昇や ドル高 ・ 円安の歪みを、 他国の政策変更によって補
正しようとする 「責任転嫁のレ ト リ ッ ク」 という側面を拭え

ない。 本当にグローバル経済の安定化を目指すのであれ
ば、 批判されるべきは日本の金融 ・ 財政政策というより
も、 景気回復期にあっても巨額の財政赤字を続ける米国
自身の 「構造的リフレ依存」 であると言える。 日本政府 ・ 日
銀としては、 米国からの外圧に過剰に動揺することなく、
自国の自律的な賃金 ・ 物価循環に基づいた主体的かつ着
実な政策を推進することが求められる。
結論と示唆
経済調査部　首席エコノミス ト　永濱 利廣（ながはま としひろ）

的⼆轮效应⻛险；增⻓⻛险偏下⾏、通胀⻛险偏上⾏， “ ⾃我们上次会议以来，融资条件已

摘要：

拉加德在开场声明中直⾔不讳地指出了当前的宏观⽭盾： “ 能源价格继续上涨，对通胀前景构成
上⾏⻛险，对增⻓前景构成下⾏⻛险。 ”

显著升⾄ 9 ⽉的 3.8% ，其中能源价格通胀从 14.3% 飙升⾄ 18.9% 。剔除能源的核⼼通胀也从 2.1%
⼩幅攀升⾄ 2.3% 。

### Page 3

下午好。我和副⾏⻓欢迎各位参加我们的新闻发布会。
管理委员会今天决定维持欧洲央⾏三⼤关键利率不变。
能源价格继续上涨，对通胀前景构成上⾏⻛险，对增⻓前景构成下⾏⻛险。尽管能源冲击的全⾯
影响仍在显现，但迄今为⽌基础通胀⼤体稳定，⼯资在此阶段也未显⽰出实质性反应。
能源冲击的幅度和持续时间增加，以及对间接效应和⼆轮效应的影响，可能推⾼通胀并压低增
⻓。
⾃我们上次会议以来，融资条件已经收紧，这可能拖累通胀和增⻓。我们致⼒于制定货币政策，
以确保通胀在中期稳定在 2% 的⽬标。
我们仍处于有利位置，能够应对当前⾼度不确定性。我们将采⽤依赖数据、逐次会议的⽅式来确
定适当的货币政策⽴场。特别是，我们的利率决策将基于我们对通胀前景及其周围⻛险的评估，
并结合即将公布的经济和⾦融数据、基础通胀动态以及货币政策传导⼒度。我们不预先承诺特定
的利率路径。
今天作出的决定已在我们⽹站发布的新闻稿中列明。
我现在将更详细地阐述我们如何看待经济和通胀的发展，然后解释我们对⾦融和货币条件的评
估。
经济活动
最新数据表明，欧元区经济在第三季度继续扩张。服务业在中东冲突初期⾛弱后已经复苏。制造

业、数字服务和出⼝正受益于⼈⼯智能相关商业投资。政府在基础设施和国防⽅⾯的⽀出也在⽀
撑经济活动。这些因素应继续⽀撑近期增⻓。与此同时，能源价格重新上涨打击了消费者信⼼，
融资成本上升可能拖累增⻓。
8 ⽉失业率仍为 6.4% 。就业和劳动⼒增⻓仍然低迷，⽽⽣产率已逐步回升。
中期内，能源价格逐步下降和劳动⼒市场稳健所推动的信⼼和实际收⼊复苏，应会提振国内需
求。⼈⼯智能相关活动投资以及政府在基础设施和国防⽅⾯的⽀出也应继续⽀持增⻓。随着外需
增强，出⼝增⻓应会加快。
提⾼潜在增⻓需要结构性改⾰，并且必须以健全的公共财政为⽀撑。财政可持续性是更⼴泛经济
稳定的关键锚。对能源冲击的财政应对应是临时性、有针对性和量⾝定制的。简化并协调欧盟单
⼀市场规则、加快能源转型以及完成储蓄与投资联盟，是使我们的经济更有活⼒和韧性的关键基
⽯。随着数字欧元法律框架的达成进程进⼊最后阶段，我们重申尽快就单⼀货币⼀揽⼦⽅案达成
协议的重要性。
通胀
通胀从 8 ⽉的 3.2% 升⾄ 9 ⽉的 3.8% 。能源价格通胀显著上升，从 8 ⽉的 14.3% 升⾄ 18.9% 。柴油和

汽油价格在 9 ⽉创下新⾼，部分反映了炼油利润率上升。剔除能源的通胀从 8 ⽉的 2.1% 升⾄ 9 ⽉的
2.3% 。⻝品价格通胀从 1.1% 升⾄ 1.3% 。服务通胀从 8 ⽉的 3.0% 升⾄ 9 ⽉的 3.2% ，⽽商品通胀从
1.2% 降⾄ 1.1% 。
基础通胀指标⼤体稳定，但定价链早期阶段显⽰出上⾏压⼒。⼯资压⼒仍然温和。第⼆季度雇员
⼈均薪酬增⻓ 3.3% ，低于第⼀季度的 3.6% ，⽽⽣产率上升有助于抑制单位劳动⼒成本增⻓。我
们的前瞻性⼯资追踪指标显⽰， 2026 年剩余⽉份协商⼯资增⻓将在 2.5% ⾄ 2.8% 之间波动，并在

### Page 4

⻛险提⽰及免责条款
2027 年前三个季度平均为 2.6% 。较短期限的通胀预期已经上升，但多数⻓期通胀预期指标仍约
为 2% ，⽀持中期通胀稳定在⽬标附近。
能源价格涨幅已超过欧洲央⾏⼯作⼈员 9 ⽉预测中的基线路径。这可能在近期进⼀步推⾼通胀，
并使整体通胀在未来⼏个季度远⾼于⽬标。此后，能源通胀下降以及更⾼利率的影响，应⽀持通
胀回归⽬标。
⻛险评估
增⻓前景的⻛险偏向下⾏。这尤其源于中东冲突以及俄罗斯对乌克兰不正当战争的最新事态发
展。能源供应持续中断可能导致能源价格进⼀步上涨，且上涨时间⽐⽬前预期更⻓。这将拖累实
际收⼊、⽀出和投资。全球⾦融市场情绪恶化或全球债券市场溢出效应加剧，可能收紧融资条
件，从⽽抑制需求。财政不确定性也可能进⼀步收紧融资条件并削弱信⼼，从⽽降低消费和投
资。主要经济体之间贸易紧张局势重燃，可能进⼀步扰乱供应链、减少出⼝并削弱消费和投资。
相反，如果经济和能源市场⽐预期更快地适应持续冲突造成的扰动，或者这些冲突得到可持续解
决，增⻓可能⾼于预期。此外，欧元区企业采⽤新技术、政府在基础设施和国防⽅⾯的⽀出，以

及提⾼⽣产率和完成欧盟单⼀市场的改⾰，可能⽐预期更多地推动增⻓。
通胀前景的⻛险偏向上⾏。这尤其源于中东冲突以及俄罗斯对乌克兰不正当战争的最新事态发
展。能源冲击可能进⼀步加剧，其对其他价格和⼯资的影响可能⽐⽬前预期更强。尤其是天然⽓
价格，如果供应进⼀步中断，或者异常寒冷的冬季恰逢低库存⽔平，可能上涨。能源价格保持⾼
位的时间越⻓，就越可能通过间接效应和⼆轮效应推⾼更⼴泛的通胀。贸易紧张局势重燃可能导
致全球供应链更加碎⽚化，削减关键原材料供应，并加剧欧元区经济的产能约束。极端天⽓事件
—— 可能因 “ 厄尔尼诺 ” 条件加剧⽽强化 —— 以及更⼴泛的⽓候和⾃然危机持续发展，可能⽐预期
更多地推⾼⻝品价格。相反，如果持续的地缘政治冲突得到可持续解决，或者能源价格上涨带来
的间接效应或⼆轮效应⽐预期更不明显，通胀可能低于预期。更加波动且⻛险厌恶的⾦融市场，
加上更⾼的⻓期利率，可能拖累需求，从⽽降低通胀。
⾦融和货币条件
⾃我们上次会议以来，⾦融条件已经收紧。利率普遍上升，⽽股价下跌。
企业市场债务成本从 7 ⽉的 4.0% 升⾄ 8 ⽉的 4.1% ，⽽银⾏对企业贷款利率仍为 3.8% 。银⾏对企业

贷款增⻓从 7 ⽉的 4.4% 放缓⾄ 8 ⽉和 9 ⽉的年率 4.2% ，⽽公司债券发⾏在 9 ⽉以年率 4.2% 增⻓，
⾼于 8 ⽉的 3.8% 和 7 ⽉的 3.5% 。正如我们最新银⾏贷款调查所报告，第三季度对企业贷款的信贷
标准收紧，银⾏称担⼼其客⼾⾯临的经济⻛险。尽管库存和营运资⾦的融资需求上升，企业贷款
需求仍下降，尤其反映了更⾼利率的影响。
抵押贷款利率从 7 ⽉的 3.5% 升⾄ 8 ⽉的 3.6% ，⽽抵押贷款增⻓在 9 ⽉⼩幅降⾄ 3.0% 。第三季度抵
押贷款信贷标准保持不变，⽽由于利率上升，抵押贷款需求⼤幅下降。
结论
管理委员会今天决定维持欧洲央⾏三⼤关键利率不变。我们致⼒于制定货币政策，以确保通胀在
中期稳定在 2% 的⽬标。我们将采⽤依赖数据、逐次会议的⽅式来确定适当的货币政策⽴场。我
们的利率决策将基于我们对通胀前景及其周围⻛险的评估，并结合即将公布的经济和⾦融数据、
基础通胀动态以及货币政策传导⼒度。我们不预先承诺特定的利率路径。
⽆论如何，我们准备在职责范围内调整所有⼯具，以确保通胀可持续地稳定在我们的中期⽬标，

并维护货币政策传导的顺畅运⾏。

### Page 5

市场有⻛险，投资需谨慎。本⽂不构成个⼈投资建议，也未考虑到个别⽤⼾特殊的投资⽬标、财务状况或需要。⽤⼾应考虑本⽂
中的任何意⻅、观点或结论是否符合其特定状况。据此投资，责任⾃负。

ユーロ圏の物価・金利・失業率見通し（％）
（四半期）（資料）Eurostat、Ｄａｔａｓｔｒｅａｍ、ニッセイ基礎研究所
見通しＨＩＣＰ上昇率（前年比）
独10年金利
ＥＣＢ預金ファシリティ金利
失業率
１. 中東情勢の緩和が見通せないなかで、エネルギー価格が高止まりしている。原油価格の
ほか一部の石油製品価格や天然ガス価格の上昇もインフレ圧力に大きく寄与している。 
２. 4-6 月期のユーロ圏の実質成長率は前期比0.6％（年率2.6％、前期▲0.0％）となった。
足もとアイルランドの多国籍企業活動の寄与で成長率が大きく振れているが、アイルラ
ンドを除く成長率は前期比0.3％（年率1.2％、前期0.3％）となり緩やかな成長基調が
維持された。中東情勢緊迫化の悪影響は1-3 月期に続き 4-6 月期でも限定的だった。 
３. ＨＩＣＰ（速報値）は、8 月の前年比伸び率は、総合指数で3.3％、エネルギー・飲食料
を除くコア指数で同 2.4％となった。エネルギーインフレは前年比 14.3％まで上昇して
おり、総合指数を大幅に押し上げている。一方で、コア指数は財インフレが上昇してい
るものの、サービスインフレが低下しており横ばい圏で推移している。 
４. ＥＣＢはエネルギー価格の上昇を受けて 6 月会合に続き 9 月会合でも利上げを実施し
た。一方で、先行きについては不確実性が高い状況下で政策金利経路は予測できないと
して、引き続き会合毎にデータ次第で金融政策決定を行う姿勢を強調した。 
５. 今後の見通しは、中東情勢が徐々に改善に向かいエネルギー価格も緩やかに下落すると
の前提のもと、成長率は緩やかな成長基調を維持すると予想する（成長率：26 年 1.0％、
27 年1.2％）。インフレ率は当面は目標を上回るものの、 先行きはエネルギー価格の下落
に合わせて再び目標に向けて緩やかに低下すると予想する（26 年3.0％、27 年 2.5％）。
ＥＣＢはインフレ圧力が継続することを受け年内に追加利上げを実施すると予想する。 
６. 成長率のリスクは下振れ、インフレ率のリスクは上振れに傾いている。エネルギー価格
の高止まりは 2 次的効果によって高インフレが定着し、ＥＣＢに積極的な利上げを促す
可能性がある。また、交易条件の悪化で所得流出を通じた成長率の下振れ要因となる。 
ニッセイ基礎研究所 2026-09-16 
欧州経済見通し 
－エネルギー高の中でも底堅い成長を維持

経済研究部 常務理事 伊藤 さゆり 
(03)3512-1832 ito@nli-research.co.jp

経済研究部 主任研究員 高山 武士 
(03)3512-1818  takayama@nli-research.co.jp

6.4％増となっている（図表 5）。中東を含む西アジアからの輸入数量（季節調整値）は6 月に前年

た。1-3 月期の低成長と4-6 月期の反発は多国籍企業の活動によるアイルランドの落ち込みと反発

輸出は、貿易統計から国別の輸出数量を確認すると、 足もと米国向けの輸出が持ち直しているこ
とに加え、 これまで低迷していた中国向けの輸出も下げ止まっていることなどが伸びをけん引した
（図表 10）。米国はＥＵに対して引き続き高めの関税を課しているものの5、ユーロ圏の対米輸出（金
額ベース）はこれまで輸出を押し下げてきた医薬品・有機化学品のマイナス寄与が縮小し、また一
部機械類・電子機器が輸出の改善に寄与した（図表11）。

（図表 12）

個人消費デフレータが大幅に上昇した。また、 輸入デフレータ上昇率が前期比で3.6％ （前期 ：1.1％） 、

表12）。

5 米国のＥＵに対する関税は品目別関税のほか、国際緊急経済権限法（ＩＥＥＰＡ）に基づく相互関税に違憲判決が下されたことを受
けて26 年2 月24 日から7 月 24 日まで相互関税の後継として通商法122 条に基づく課徴金10％が課されていた。その後、7 月24 日以
降は通商法301 条に基づく関税（強制労働の使用に対する関税）として10％の関税が賦課されている（60 か国・地域が対象） 。

（図表 14）

ユーロ圏は中東紛争激化によるエネルギー高という逆風下ではあるが、総じて安定した成長を達
成していると言える。

（ 雇用環境は底堅いが、実質賃金の伸びは減速 ） 
雇用環境は底堅い。 
失業率は 6％台前半で低位安定する一方、製造業・サービス業ともに人手不足感の緩和が続いて
いる（図表 15、欧州委員会調査）。4-6 月期のユーロ圏の労働投入の伸び率は、就業者数が前期比
0.1％（前期：0.1％） 、雇用者数が前期比0.2％（前期：0.0％） 、労働時間（就業者数ベース）が前
期比 0.1％（前期：▲0.1％）となった。実質成長率と比較して労働投入の伸びは抑制されており、
労働生産性は改善している。 
一方、実質賃金総額 （社会保障給付除く） の伸び率は前年比0.9％となり（図表 16）、 時間当たり
賃金が緩やかに減速するなかで、 個人消費デフレータが上昇したため、23 年 4-6 月 （前年比0.4％）
以来の低い伸び率となった。 雇用環境は底堅いものの、実質所得の減速により消費のけん引力は低
下している。 
（図表 15）

（図表 16）

コア部分の内訳を見ると、エネルギーを除く財が緩やかに上昇し、8 月は前年比 1.2％と 24 年 2
月（同 1.5％）以来の高さまで上昇した。一方、サービスは同3.0％となり22 年 3 月（同 2.7％）
以来の低さに低下した（図表18）。 
財インフレについて、上流の輸入物価指数や生産者物価指数を見ると、エネルギー関連以外の業
種でも上昇率が高まっている。化学品や金属などエネルギー集約型の中間財でインフレ圧力が強ま
っているほか、世界的なＡＩ関連品目の需要増を受けてコンピュータ・電子・光学製品関連の価格
上昇も見られる（図表 19）。下流のＨＩＣＰでも情報通信関連の一部品目で価格が上昇している。
サービスインフレについて、連動が強い賃金上昇率を見ると、低下基調は維持されているが、先行
指標（求人賃金や妥結済みの賃金動向を集計したＥＣＢの賃金トラッカー）については、下げ止ま
りの兆しが見られる（図表20）。

（ 財政政策：財政スタンスは 26 年に緩和、27 年以降は再び中立に ）

やエネルギー対策の一部が財政ルールから除外されることもあり 6、やや緩和的になると見込まれ

6 防衛関連支出（上限はＧＤＰ比1.5％）を財政ルールの適用から除外する国家免責条項がユーロ圏14 か国（ＥＵでは18 か国）に発
動されている。ユーロ圏加盟国ではブルガリア、ベルギー、エストニア、ギリシャ、クロアチア、ラトビア、リトアニア、ポルトガ
ル、スロベニア、スロバキア、フィンランド、ドイツ、オーストリア、スペイン（非ユーロ圏加盟国ではチェコ、デンマーク、ハンガ

（ 金融政策・金利：ＥＣＢは段階的に利上げ ） 
ＥＣＢは 24 年央から制限的な金融政策からの緩和を開始し、25 年 6 月にかけて政策金利（預金
ファシリティ金利）を 4.0％から 2.0％まで引き下げた。その後、中東紛争の激化とエネルギー価
格上昇を受けて、 ＥＣＢは6 月会合および9 月会合で利上げを決定した（図表 22、直近の利上げは
9 月 16 日から適用予定のため、図表には反映されていない） 。政策金利は現在 2.5％とＥＣＢが推
計する中立金利レンジ（2.0-2.5％9）の上限水準となっている。 
ＥＣＢはエネルギーショックによるインフレ圧力に警戒感を強めているが、9 月会合では不確実
性が高い状況下であることから、 今後の政策金利経路については予測できないとし、政策金利決定
はインフレ見通しとそれを取り巻くリスク、 基調的なインフレ率の動向、 金融政策の伝達の強さの
評価に基づいて会合毎にデータ次第で決定するという従来の姿勢を強調している。 
今後の政策については、エネルギーショックの強度と期間、ショックの間接的効果（エネルギー
以外の品目へのエネルギーショックの波及） や2 次的効果（賃金とインフレ率の相互作用によるイ
ンフレ定着）の度合いが判断され、エネルギー価格の長期の高止まりや上昇、間接的効果の増大、
2 次的効果の顕在化などが見られれば、さらに追加利上げが行われると見られる。

ユーロ圏の長期金利は、引き続き金融政策やインフレ関連データ、財政スタンス、世界的な金利
動向に左右される展開となっている。 中東情勢に改善の兆しが見えないなか、インフレ懸念やＥＣ

Member States on Broadening the Scope of the National Escape Clause for Energy Security（26 年9 月15 日アクセス） 。
